Stockholder Matters</P>

<BR>
<P style="page-break-before:always; margin-top:14pt; margin-bottom:3.5pt; line-height:16pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:14pt" align=center><B>PART I</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-14pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>ITEM 1.</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:72pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>DESCRIPTION OF BUSINESS.</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>General</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">St. Joseph Capital Corporation (the &#147;Company&#148;) is a bank holding company that owns St. Joseph Capital Bank (the &#147;Bank&#148;), a full-service commercial banking institution headquartered in Mishawaka, Indiana with a full service banking center located in Elkhart, Indiana. &nbsp;The Bank provides a broad range of banking products and services, including credit, cash management and deposits to its targeted client base of executives and professionals and small- to medium-sized businesses. &nbsp;The Bank&#146;s market area encompasses a substantial portion of the Indiana communities of Mishawaka, South Bend, Notre Dame, Granger, Elkhart, Goshen, Bristol, Middlebury, Osceola, Nappanee, Wakarusa, Michigan City, and LaPorte as well as certain Michigan communities, including Niles, Edwardsburg, and Cassopolis. &nbsp;Due to the overlap of this metropolitan area over state lines, this region is often referred to as &#147;Michiana.&#148; &nbsp;At December 31, 2005, the Company had total assets of $481.1 million, loans of $335.1 million and deposits of $343.4 million.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company started its banking operations on February 13, 1997 with the goal of building a locally owned and managed financial institution to meet the banking needs of the Bank&#146;s targeted clients. &nbsp;As part of its operating strategy, the Company strives to offer clients an extraordinary level of service on a consistent basis. &nbsp;The Company&#146;s growth since 1997 largely has been a product of its success in attracting targeted individuals and businesses to become clients ,
 coupled with the ability to recruit and retain a community-oriented management team with significant commercial banking experience in the Michiana area. &nbsp;The Company has also taken advantage of the client disruption caused by the acquisition of a number of the area&#146;s other locally owned financial institutions by large and super-regional bank holding companies. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company&#146;s rapid growth, coupled with the desire to increase market share, led the Company to raise additional capital in 1999. &nbsp;This was accomplished through a common stock offering. &nbsp;The growth experienced to date, as well as continued growth, will allow the Company to take greater advantage of the operating leverage available to larger financial institutions. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"> From inception until April 2005
, the Company and Bank ha d
 operated out of a single location as a way of managing their fixed overhead expenses. &nbsp;The delivery of banking services ha d
 been accomplished through the Bank&#146;s headquarters facility in Mishawaka, Indiana, a courier service program, an ATM/debit card product, the postage paid bank-by-mail program, telephone banking, and Internet banking. &nbsp;This variety of delivery systems enabled the Bank&#146;s clients to choose the method of banking which is most convenient for them. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">In April of 2005 the Company expanded into Elkhart, Indiana with the opening of a new banking center. &nbsp;This expansion started a new era in the life of St. Joseph Capital Corporation. &nbsp;The decision to expand was a natural yet strategic progression for the Company since the single location in Mishawaka, Indiana was being fully utilized and the Elkhart market presents the Company with significant marketing opportunities. &nbsp;The new Elkhart banking center is located approximately 20 miles from the Mishawaka location and occupies the first floor of a three story office building in
 downtown Elkhart. &nbsp;The vast majority of the employees used to staff the new banking center were transferred from the Mishawaka location. &nbsp;The Company&#146;s presence in this market will provide additional opportunity for new relationships and business. &nbsp;In the future, the Bank may also expand its product offerings, move into other new markets, or make strategic acquisitions of other financial institutions. &nbsp;The Bank intends to continue to pursue an aggressive growth strategy focused on the addition of experienced banking personnel in the Michiana area, while also maintaining strong asset quality and enhancing its profitability.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank has and will continue to price its loan products and deposit products competitively. &nbsp;The net interest income generated by the lending and deposit-gathering activities of the Bank should continue to increase as the overall size of the Bank increases. &nbsp;For additional discussion related to net interest income see the Management&#146;s Discussion and Analysis of Financial Condition and Results<BR>
of Operation section of the document.
</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company&#146;s headquarters is currently maintained in their location at 3820 Edison Lakes Parkway, Mishawaka, Indiana, 46545. &nbsp;The telephone number is (574) 273-9700.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Business Strategy</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank expects to establish and maintain a high standard of quality in each service that is provided, and its employees are expected to emphasize a consulting and service oriented approach in their dealings with clients. &nbsp;The use of technology has and will continue to permit each employee to devote more time and attention to personal service, respond more quickly to client requests, and deliver services in the most timely manner possible.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank&#146;s goal is to create and maintain a &#147;client-driven&#148; organization focused on providing high value to clients by promptly delivering products and services matched directly to their needs. &nbsp;Furthermore, the Bank expects to gain market share by developing strong ties to the community. &nbsp;In this regard, most of the Company&#146;s directors currently hold, and have held in the past, leadership positions in a number of community organizations, and intend to continue this active involvement in future years. &nbsp;Members of the management team also hold leadership positions in a variety of community organizations and intend to continue this active involvement in future years. &nbsp;Additionally, all employees are encouraged to be active in the civic, charitable, and social organizations located in the Michiana area.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank offers a broad range of deposit services, including checking accounts, money market accounts, savings accounts, and time deposits of various types, as well as a full range of short- to intermediate-term personal and commercial loans. &nbsp;Commercial loans include lines of credit to finance accounts receivable and inventory, term loans to finance machinery and equipment, as well as commercial mortgages to finance land and buildings. &nbsp;The Bank makes personal loans directly to its clients for various purposes, including purchases of automobiles, boats, and other recreational vehicles, home improvements, education, and personal investments. &nbsp;The Bank also makes residential mortgage loans and until 2006 substantially all of them were retained by the Bank and consist of balloon payment, adjustable and fixed rate mortgages. &nbsp;In 2006, the Bank will begin to offer a saleable servicing released mortgage product for longer term fixed rate residential mortgage loans. &nbsp;The Bank offers other services, including credit cards, cashier&#146;s checks, traveler&#146;s checks, and automated teller access.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=justify><B>Lending Activities</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B></B>The Bank provides a broad range of commercial and retail lending services to corporations, partnerships, and individuals , primarily in the Michiana area
. &nbsp;The Bank actively markets its services to qualified borrowers. &nbsp;Lending officers actively solicit the business of new borrowers entering the Bank&#146;s market area as well as long-standing members of the local business community. &nbsp;Management has established lending policies, which include a number of underwriting factors to be considered in making a loan, including location, loan to value ratio, cash flow, interest rate, and the credit history of the borrower.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank&#146;s legal lending limit is approximately $5.9 million. &nbsp;The board of directors, however, has established an &#147;in-house&#148; limit of up to approximately $4.7 million depending on the credit quality of the client. &nbsp;The board may from time to time raise or lower the &#147;in-house&#148; limit, as it deems appropriate to comply with safe and sound banking practices and in response to overall economic conditions.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B></B>As part of the loan monitoring activities at the Bank, the officers loan committee meets weekly to review loan portfolio dynamics. &nbsp;Loan review officers present detailed reports on the loan portfolio to the board for their review. &nbsp;A directors loan committee meets periodically to review more significant loans and to assist the board in their loan monitoring and oversight. &nbsp;Management has attempted to identify problem loans at an early stage and to aggressively seek a resolution of these situations.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Commercial Loans</I></B>. &nbsp;The Bank is<B> </B>an active commercial lender in the Michiana area. &nbsp;The areas of emphasis include, but are not limited to, loans to manufacturers, building contractors, developers and business services companies. &nbsp;The Bank also provides a wide range of operating loans, including lines of credit for working capital and operational purposes, as well as term loans for the acquisition of equipment and other purposes. &nbsp;Collateral for these loans generally includes accounts receivable, inventory, equipment, and real estate. &nbsp;In addition, the Bank has taken personal guarantees to help assure payment when appropriate. &nbsp;Terms of commercial business loans generally range from one to five years. &nbsp;A portion of the Bank&#146;s commercial business loans has floating interest rates or re-price within one year. &nbsp;The Bank also makes commercial real estate loans, which are generally secured by the underlying real estate and improvements. &nbsp;However, management may also require additional assets of the borrower as collateral.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Residential Real Estate Mortgage Loans</I>. &nbsp;</B> Although the
main focus of the Bank&#146;s lending activity continues to be on commercial loans,
management views
 residential mortgages as a way to attract and service targeted clients. &nbsp;Currently ,
 the Bank offers a variety of adjustable rate products and newly offered fixed rate saleable products. &nbsp;Additional mortgage products may be introduced, as management deems necessary. &nbsp;The growth in the residential loan portfolio has been a result of refinancing activities and acquisition and construction of existing or new homes. &nbsp;The Bank has retained substantially all its adjustable rate residential real estate loans in its portfolio and intends to continue to do so in the future. &nbsp;Beginning in 2006 s
ubstantially all new long term fixed rate products will be sold with the servicing released to the secondary market.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Consumer Lending</I>. &nbsp;</B>The Bank provides all types of consumer loans including motor vehicle, home improvement, home equity, signature loans, and small personal credit lines. &nbsp;The Bank has no indirect lending, and intends to actively seek to increase its personal lines of credit and home equity loans.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Competition</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank&#146;s market area is competitive. &nbsp;It competes for loans principally through the range and quality of service it provides and interest rates. &nbsp;The Bank&#146;s reputation in the communities it serves and personal service philosophy enhances its ability to compete favorably in attracting and retaining individual and business clients. &nbsp;The Bank actively solicits deposits by offering clients personal attention, professional service and competitive rates.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">There are other commercial banks, savings and loans, and credit unions, along with other financial institutions that operate in the Bank&#146;s primary market area. &nbsp;In addition, many other financial institutions based in the communities surrounding these areas also actively compete for clients within the Company&#146;s market area. &nbsp;The Bank also faces competition from finance companies, insurance companies, mortgage companies, securities brokerage firms, money market mutual funds, loan production offices, and other providers of financial services.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=justify><B>Market Area</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank offers a full range of commercial and consumer banking services primarily within a thirty-mile radius of the main office located in Mishawaka, Indiana and the new banking center in Elkhart, Indiana. &nbsp;This area encompasses a substantial portion of the Indiana communities of Mishawaka, South Bend, Notre Dame, Granger, Elkhart, Goshen, Bristol, Middlebury, Osceola, Nappanee, Wakarusa, Michigan City, and LaPorte as well as certain Michigan communities, including Niles, Edwardsburg, and Cassopolis. &nbsp;Because of the overlap of this metropolitan area over state lines, this region is often referred to as &#147;Michiana.&#148;</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The local economy is diversified among manufacturing, retail and wholesale trades and service industries. &nbsp;The area&#146;s retail, distribution, convention and tourism, health care and service sectors have expanded to offset the decrease in manufacturing jobs, which has occurred in recent years. &nbsp;The University of Notre Dame is the area&#146;s largest employer and contributes to the stability of the local economy. &nbsp;South Bend is also home to other colleges and technical schools providing additional stability and access to a skilled work force.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Memorial Health System and St.&nbsp;Joseph&#146;s Regional Medical Center are the two largest health care providers in the Michiana area and are also major employers. &nbsp;AM General Corp., Honeywell, and Bayer Corporation are three of the region&#146;s largest manufacturers, and the area is also home to a number of smaller manufacturing, retail, and service businesses. &nbsp;Many major manufacturing companies are also located in adjacent Elkhart County, including Keystone RV Company, Forest River Industries, Inc., and Coachmen Industries. &nbsp;This diverse commercial base provides significant potential for business and personal banking services for owners and employees of these entities.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=justify><B>Employees</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank has approximately 79 full-time equivalent employees. &nbsp;None of the employees are covered by a collective bargaining agreement with the Bank. &nbsp;Management considers employee relations to be excellent.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Internet Website</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank maintains an Internet site at www.sjcb.com. &nbsp;On this site, the Company&#146;s annual report on Form 10-K, quarterly reports on Form 10-Q, current reports on Form 8-K, and other reports filed or furnished pursuant to Section 13(a) or 15(d) of the Exchange Act , as well as any amendments thereto,
 are posted as soon as reasonably practicable after it electronically files such material with, or furnishes it to, the Securities and Exchange Commission and are available free of charge. &nbsp;The filings are also available on the Securities and Exchange Commission&#146;s website at www.sec.gov. &nbsp;Additionally, the Company will provide copies of its filings free of charge upon written request to: Mark E. Secor, Senior Vice President, Chief Financial Officer, St. Joseph Capital Corporation, 3820 Edison Lakes Parkway, Mishawaka, Indiana 46545.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company has a code of business conduct and ethics in place that applies to all of its directors and employees. &nbsp;The code sets forth the standard of ethics that the Company expects all of its directors and employees to follow, including the Chief Executive Officer and Chief Financial Officer. &nbsp;The code of business conduct and ethics is posted on the Company&#146;s website at www.sjcb.com (and is filed as an exhibit to the Company&#146;s Annual Report on
Form 10-K). &nbsp;The Company intends to satisfy the disclosure requirements under Item 10 of Form 8-K regarding any amendment to or waiver of the code with respect to the Chief Executive Officer and Chief Financial Officer, and persons performing similar functions, by posting such information on its website.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Supervision and Regulation</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B><I>General</I></B><I>. &nbsp;</I>Financial institutions, their holding companies and their affiliates are extensively regulated under federal and state law. &nbsp;As a result, the growth and earnings performance of the Company may be affected not only by management decisions and general economic conditions, but also by the requirements of federal and state statutes and by the regulations and policies of various bank regulatory authorities, including the Indiana Department of Financial Institutions (the &#147;DFI&#148;), the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System (the&nbsp;&#147;Federal&nbsp;Reserve&#148;) and the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (the &#147;FDIC&#148;). &nbsp;Furthermore, taxation laws administered by the Internal Revenue Service and state taxing authorities and securities laws administered by the Securities and Exchange Commission (the&nbsp;&#147;SEC&#148;) and state securities authorities have an impact on the business of the Company. The effect of these statutes, regulations and regulatory policies may be significant, and cannot be predicted with a high degree of certainty.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt">Federal and state laws and regulations generally applicable to financial institutions regulate, among other things, the scope of business, the kinds and amounts of investments, reserve requirements, capital levels relative to operations, the nature and amount of collateral for loans, the establishment of branches, mergers and consolidations and the payment of dividends. This system of supervision and regulation establishes a comprehensive framework for the respective operations of the Company and its subsidiaries and is intended primarily for the protection of the FDIC-insured deposits and depositors of the Bank, rather than stockholders
.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The following is a summary of the material elements of the regulatory framework that applies to the Company and its subsidiaries. &nbsp;It does not describe all of the statutes, regulations and regulatory policies that apply, nor does it restate all of the requirements of those that are described. As such, the following is qualified in its entirety by reference to applicable law. &nbsp;Any&nbsp;change in statutes, regulations or regulatory policies may have a material effect on the business of the Company and its subsidiaries.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>General.</I></B><I> &nbsp;</I>The Company, as the sole shareholder of the Bank, is a bank holding company. &nbsp;As a bank holding company, the Company is registered with, and is subject to regulation by, the Federal Reserve under the Bank Holding Company Act of 1956, as amended (the &#147;BHCA&#148;). &nbsp;In&nbsp;accordance with Federal Reserve policy, the Company is expected to act as a source of financial strength to the Bank and to commit resources to support the Bank in circumstances where the Company might not otherwise do so. &nbsp;Under the BHCA, the Company is subject to periodic examination by the Federal Reserve. &nbsp;The Company is required to file with the Federal&nbsp;Reserve periodic reports of the Company&#146;s operations and such additional information regarding the Company and its subsidiaries as the Federal Reserve may require. &nbsp;The Company is also subject to regulation by the DFI under Indiana law.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Acquisitions, Activities and Change in Control.</I> &nbsp;</B>The primary purpose of a bank holding company is to control and manage banks.<B> </B>&nbsp;The BHCA generally requires the prior approval of the Federal Reserve for any merger involving a bank holding company or any acquisition by a bank holding company of another bank or bank holding company. &nbsp;Subject to certain conditions (including deposit concentration limits established by the BHCA), the Federal Reserve may allow a bank holding company to acquire banks located in any state of the United States. In&nbsp;approving interstate acquisitions, the Federal Reserve is required to give effect to applicable state law limitations on the aggregate amount of deposits that may be held by the acquiring bank holding company and its insured depository institution affiliates in the state in which the target bank is located (provided that those limits do not discriminate against out-of-state depository institutions or their holding companies) and state laws that require that the target bank have been in existence for a minimum period of time (not to exceed five years) before being acquired by an out-of-state bank holding company. </P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The BHCA generally prohibits the Company from acquiring direct or indirect ownership or control of more than 5% of the voting shares of any company that is not a bank and from engaging in any business other than that of banking, managing and controlling banks or furnishing services to banks and their subsidiaries. &nbsp;This general prohibition is subject to a number of exceptions. The principal exception allows bank holding companies to engage in, and to own shares of companies engaged in, certain businesses found by the Federal Reserve to be &#147;so closely related to banking ... as to be a proper incident thereto.&#148; &nbsp;This authority would permit the Company to engage in a variety of banking-related businesses, including the operation of a thrift, consumer finance, equipment leasing, the operation of a computer service bureau (including software development), and mortgage banking and brokerage. The BHCA generally does not place territorial restrictions on the domestic activities of non-bank subsidiaries of bank holding companies.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Additionally, bank holding companies that meet certain eligibility requirements prescribed by the BHCA and elect to operate as financial holding companies may engage in, or own shares in companies engaged in, a wider range of nonbanking activities, including securities and insurance underwriting and sales, merchant banking and any other activity that the Federal&nbsp;Reserve, in consultation with the Secretary of the Treasury, determines by regulation or order is financial in nature, incidental to any such financial activity or complementary to any such financial activity and does not pose a substantial risk to the safety or soundness of depository institutions or the financial system generally. &nbsp;As of the date of this filing, the Company has not applied for approval to operate as a financial holding company.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Federal law also prohibits any person or company from acquiring &#147;control&#148; of an FDIC-insured depository institution or its holding company without prior notice to the appropriate federal bank regulator. &nbsp;&#147;Control&#148; is conclusively presumed to exist upon the acquisition of 25% or more of the outstanding voting securities of a bank or bank holding company, but may arise under certain circumstances at 10% ownership.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Capital Requirements.</I></B> &nbsp;Bank holding companies are required to maintain minimum levels of capital in accordance with Federal Reserve capital adequacy guidelines. &nbsp;If capital levels fall below the minimum required levels, a bank holding company, among other things, may be denied approval to acquire or establish additional banks or non-bank businesses. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Federal Reserve&#146;s capital guidelines establish the following minimum regulatory capital requirements for bank holding companies: (i) a risk-based requirement expressed as a percentage of total assets weighted according to risk; and (ii) a leverage requirement expressed as a percentage of total assets. &nbsp;The risk-based requirement consists of a minimum ratio of total capital to total risk-weighted assets of 8% and a minimum ratio of Tier 1 capital to total risk-weighted assets of 4%. &nbsp;The leverage requirement consists of a minimum ratio of Tier 1 capital to total assets of 3% for the most highly rated companies, with a minimum requirement of 4% for all others. &nbsp;For purposes of these capital standards, Tier 1 capital consists primarily of permanent stockholders&#146; equity less intangible assets (other than certain loan servicing rights and purchased credit card relationships). Total capital consists primarily of Tier 1 capital plus certain other debt and equity instruments that do not qualify as Tier 1 capital and a portion of the company&#146;s allowance for loan and lease losses.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The risk-based and leverage standards described above are minimum requirements. Higher capital levels will be required if warranted by the particular circumstances or risk profiles of individual banking organizations. For example, the Federal Reserve&#146;s capital guidelines contemplate that additional capital may be required to take adequate account of, among other things, interest rate risk, or the risks posed by concentrations of credit, nontraditional activities or securities trading activities. &nbsp;Further, any banking organization experiencing or anticipating significant growth would be expected to maintain capital ratios, including tangible capital positions (<I>i.e.</I>, Tier 1 capital less all intangible assets), well above the minimum levels. &nbsp;As of December 31, 2005, the Company had regulatory capital in excess of the Federal Reserve&#146;s minimum requirements.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Dividend Payments.</I></B><I> &nbsp;</I>The Company&#146;s ability to pay dividends to its shareholders may be affected by both general corporate law considerations and policies of the Federal Reserve applicable to bank holding companies<I>.</I> As a Delaware corporation, the Company is subject to the limitations of the Delaware General Corporation Law (the &#147;DGCL&#148;), which allow the Company to pay dividends only out of its surplus (as defined and computed in accordance with the provisions of the DGCL) or if the Company has no such surplus, out of its net profits for the fiscal year in which the dividend is declared and/or the preceding fiscal year. Additionally, policies of the Federal Reserve caution that a bank holding company should not pay cash dividends that exceed its net income or that can only be funded in ways that weaken the bank holding company&#146;s financial health, such as by borrowing. &nbsp;The Federal Reserve also possesses enforcement powers over bank holding companies and their non-bank subsidiaries to prevent or remedy actions that represent unsafe or unsound practices or violations of applicable statutes and regulations. &nbsp;Among these powers is the ability to proscribe the payment of dividends by banks and bank holding companies.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Federal Securities Regulation.</I></B> &nbsp;The Company&#146;s common stock is registered with the SEC under the Securities Act of 1933, as amended, and the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, as amended (the&nbsp;&#147;Exchange Act&#148;). &nbsp;Consequently, the Company is subject to the information, proxy solicitation, insider trading and other restrictions and requirements of the SEC under the Exchange Act.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=justify><B>The Bank</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>General.</I></B><I> &nbsp;</I>The Bank is an Indiana-chartered bank, the deposit accounts of which are insured by the FDIC&#146;s Bank Insurance Fund (&#147;BIF&#148;). &nbsp;As an Indiana-chartered bank, the Bank is subject to the examination, supervision, reporting and enforcement requirements of the DFI, the chartering authority for Indiana banks, and the FDIC, designated by federal law as the primary federal regulator of state-chartered, FDIC-insured banks that, like the Bank, are not members of the Federal Reserve System (&#147;non-member banks&#148;). </P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Deposit Insurance.</I></B> &nbsp;As an FDIC-insured institution, the Bank is required to pay deposit insurance premium assessments to the FDIC. &nbsp;The FDIC has adopted a risk-based assessment system under which all insured depository institutions are placed into one of nine categories and assessed insurance premiums based upon their respective levels of capital and results of supervisory evaluations. &nbsp;Institutions classified as well-capitalized (as defined by the FDIC) and considered healthy pay the lowest premium while institutions that are less than adequately capitalized (as&nbsp;defined by the FDIC) and considered of substantial supervisory concern pay the highest premium. &nbsp;Risk classification of all insured institutions is made by the FDIC for each semi-annual assessment period.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">During the year ended December 31, 2005, BIF assessments ranged from 0% of deposits to 0.27% of deposits. &nbsp;For the semi-annual assessment period beginning January 1, 2006, BIF assessment rates will continue to range from 0% of deposits to 0.27% of deposits. </P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>FICO Assessments.</I></B>&nbsp;&nbsp;Since 1987, a portion of the deposit insurance assessments paid by members of the FDIC&#146;s Savings Association Insurance Fund (&#147;SAIF&#148;) has been used to cover interest payments due on the outstanding obligations of the Financing Corporation (&#147;FICO&#148;). FICO was created in 1987 to finance the recapitalization of the Federal Savings and Loan Insurance Corporation, the SAIF&#146;s predecessor insurance fund. As a result of federal legislation enacted in 1996, beginning as of January&nbsp;1, 1997, both SAIF members and BIF members became subject to assessments to cover the interest payments on outstanding FICO obligations until the final maturity of such obligations in 2019. &nbsp;These FICO assessments are in addition to amounts assessed by the FDIC for deposit insurance. During the year ended December&nbsp;31, 2005, the FICO assessment rate for BIF and SAIF members was approximately 0.01% of deposits.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Supervisory Assessments.</I></B> &nbsp;All Indiana banks are required to pay supervisory assessments to the DFI to fund the operations of the DFI. &nbsp;This assessment is calculated on the basis of an institution&#146;s total assets. &nbsp;
During the year ended December 31, 2005, the Bank paid supervisory assessments to the DFI totaling $35,000.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Capital Requirements.</I></B> &nbsp;Banks are generally required to maintain capital levels in excess of other businesses. &nbsp;The FDIC has established the following minimum capital standards for state-chartered FDIC-insured non-member banks, such as the Bank: (i) a leverage requirement consisting of a minimum ratio of Tier 1 capital to total assets of 3% for the most highly-rated banks with a minimum requirement of at least 4% for all others; and (ii) a risk-based capital requirement consisting of a minimum ratio of total capital to total risk-weighted assets of 8% and a minimum ratio of Tier 1 capital to total risk-weighted assets of 4%. In general, the components of Tier 1 capital and total capital are the same as those for bank holding companies discussed above.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The capital requirements described above are minimum requirements. &nbsp;Higher capital levels will be required if warranted by the particular circumstances or risk profiles of individual institutions. &nbsp;For example, regulations of the FDIC provide that additional capital may be required to take adequate account of, among other things, interest rate risk or the risks posed by concentrations of credit, nontraditional activities or securities trading activities.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Further, federal law and regulations provide various incentives for financial institutions to maintain regulatory capital at levels in excess of minimum regulatory requirements. &nbsp;
For example, a financial institution that is well-capitalized may qualify for exemptions from prior notice or application requirements otherwise applicable to certain types of activities and may qualify for expedited processing of other required notices or applications. &nbsp;
Additionally, one of the criteria that determine a bank holding company&#146;s eligibility to operate as a financial holding company is a requirement that all of its financial institution subsidiaries be well-capitalized.
&nbsp;Under the regulations of the FDIC, in order to be well-capitalized a financial institution must maintain a ratio of total capital to total risk-weighted assets of 10% or greater, a ratio of Tier 1 capital to total risk-weighted assets of 6% or greater and a ratio of Tier 1 capital to total assets of 5% or greater.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Federal law also provides the federal banking regulators with broad power to take prompt corrective action to resolve the problems of undercapitalized institutions. &nbsp;The extent of the regulators&#146; powers depends on whether the institution in question is adequately capitalized, undercapitalized,
significantly undercapitalized or critically undercapitalized, in each case as defined by regulation. &nbsp;Depending upon the capital category to which an institution is assigned, the regulators&#146; corrective powers include: (i) requiring the institution to submit a capital restoration plan; (ii)&nbsp;limiting the institution&#146;s asset growth and restricting its activities; (iii) requiring the institution to issue additional capital stock (including additional voting stock) or to be acquired; (iv) restricting transactions between the institution and its affiliates; (v)&nbsp;restricting the interest rate the institution may pay on deposits; (vi) ordering a new election of directors of the institution; (vii) requiring that senior executive officers or directors be dismissed; (viii) prohibiting the institution from accepting deposits from correspondent banks; (ix) requiring the institution to divest certain subsidiaries; (x) prohibiting the payment of principal or interest on subordinated debt; and (xi) ultimately, appointing a receiver for the institution.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">As of December 31, 2005: (i) the Bank was not subject to a directive from the FDIC to increase its capital to an amount in excess of the minimum regulatory capital requirements; (ii) the Bank exceeded its minimum regulatory capital requirements under FDIC capital adequacy guidelines; and (iii) the Bank was well-capitalized, as defined by FDIC regulations.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Dividend Payments.</I></B> &nbsp;The primary source of funds for the Company is dividends from the Bank. &nbsp;Indiana law prohibits the Bank from paying dividends in an amount greater than its undivided profits. The Bank is required to obtain the approval of the DFI for the payment of any dividend if the total of all dividends declared by the Bank during the calendar year, including the proposed dividend, would exceed the sum of the Bank&#146;s retained net income for the year to date combined with its retained net income for the previous two years. Indiana law defines &#147;retained net income&#148; to mean the net income of a specified period, calculated under the consolidated report of income instructions, less the total amount of all dividends declared for the specified period.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The payment of dividends by any financial institution or its holding company is affected by the requirement to maintain adequate capital pursuant to applicable capital adequacy guidelines and regulations, and a financial institution generally is prohibited from paying any dividends if, following payment thereof, the institution would be undercapitalized. &nbsp;As described above, the Bank exceeded its minimum capital requirements under applicable guidelines as of December 31, 2005. &nbsp;As of December&nbsp;31, 2005, approximately $8.7 million was available to be paid as dividends by the Bank. &nbsp;Notwithstanding the availability of funds for dividends, however, the FDIC may prohibit the payment of any dividends by the Bank if the FDIC determines such payment would constitute an unsafe or unsound practice.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Insider Transactions.</I></B> &nbsp;The Bank is subject to certain restrictions imposed by federal law on extensions of credit to the Company, on investments in the stock or other securities of the Company and the acceptance of the stock or other securities of the Company as collateral for loans made by the Bank. &nbsp;Certain limitations and reporting requirements are also placed on extensions of credit by the Bank to its directors and officers, to directors and officers of the Company, to principal shareholders of the Company and to &#147;related interests&#148; of such directors, officers and principal shareholders. &nbsp;In addition, federal law and regulations may affect the terms upon which any person who is a director or officer of the Company or the Bank or a principal shareholder of the Company may obtain credit from banks with which the Bank maintains correspondent relationships. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Safety and Soundness Standards.</I></B> &nbsp;The federal banking agencies have adopted guidelines that establish operational and managerial standards to promote the safety and soundness of federally insured depository institutions. &nbsp;The guidelines set forth standards for internal controls, information systems, internal audit systems, loan documentation, credit underwriting, interest rate exposure, asset growth, compensation, fees and benefits, asset quality and earnings.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">In general, the safety and soundness guidelines prescribe the goals to be achieved in each area, and each institution is responsible for establishing its own procedures to achieve those goals. &nbsp;If an institution fails to comply with any of the standards set forth in the guidelines, the institution&#146;s primary federal regulator may require the institution to submit a plan for achieving and maintaining compliance. If an institution fails to submit an acceptable compliance plan, or fails in any material respect to implement a compliance plan that has been accepted by its primary federal regulator, the regulator is required to issue an order directing the institution to cure the deficiency. Until the deficiency cited in the regulator&#146;s order is cured, the regulator may restrict the institution&#146;s rate of growth, require the institution to increase its capital, restrict the rates the institution pays on deposits or require the institution to take any action the regulator deems appropriate under the circumstances. Noncompliance with the standards established by the safety and soundness guidelines may also constitute grounds for other enforcement action by the federal banking regulators, including cease and desist orders and civil money penalty assessments.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Branching Authority.</I></B> &nbsp;Indiana banks, such as the Bank, have the authority under Indiana law to establish branches anywhere in the State of Indiana, subject to receipt of all required regulatory approvals.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Federal law permits state and national banks to merge with banks in other states subject to: (i)&nbsp;regulatory approval; (ii) federal and state deposit concentration limits; and (iii) state law limitations requiring the merging bank to have been in existence for a minimum period of time (not to exceed five years) prior to the merger. &nbsp;The establishment of new interstate branches or the acquisition of individual branches of a bank in another state (rather than the acquisition of an out-of-state bank in its entirety) is permitted only in those states the laws of which expressly authorize such expansion.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>State Bank Investments and Activities.</I></B> &nbsp;The Bank generally is permitted to make investments and engage in activities directly or through subsidiaries as authorized by Indiana law. &nbsp;However, under federal law and FDIC regulations, FDIC-insured state banks are prohibited, subject to certain exceptions, from making or retaining equity investments of a type, or in an amount, that are not permissible for a national bank. &nbsp;Federal law and FDIC regulations also prohibit FDIC-insured state banks and their subsidiaries, subject to certain exceptions, from engaging as principal in any activity that is not permitted for a national bank unless the bank meets, and continues to meet, its minimum regulatory capital requirements and the FDIC determines the activity would not pose a significant risk to the deposit insurance fund of which the bank is a member. &nbsp;These restrictions have not had, and are not currently expected to have, a material impact on the operations of the Bank.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Federal Reserve System.</I></B> &nbsp;Federal Reserve regulations, as presently in effect, require depository institutions to maintain non-interest earning reserves against their transaction accounts (primarily NOW and regular checking accounts), as follows: for transaction accounts aggregating $48.3 million or less, the reserve requirement is 3% of total transaction accounts; and for transaction accounts aggregating in excess of $48.3 million, the reserve requirement is $1.215 million plus 10% of the aggregate amount of total transaction accounts in excess of $48.3&nbsp;million. &nbsp;The first $7.8 million of otherwise reservable balances are exempted from the reserve requirements. &nbsp;These reserve requirements are subject to annual adjustment by the Federal Reserve. &nbsp;The Bank is in compliance with the foregoing requirements.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I> Recent Regulatory Developments
</I></B> . &nbsp;On February 8, 2006, President Bush signed the Federal Deposit Insurance Reform Act of 2005 (&#147;FDIRA&#148;) into law as part of the Deficit Reduction Act of 2005. &nbsp;On&nbsp;February&nbsp;15,&nbsp;2006, President Bush signed into law the technical and conforming amendments designed to implement FDIRA. &nbsp;FDIRA provides for legislative reforms to modernize the federal deposit insurance system.
</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"> Among other things, FDIRA: (i) merges the BIF and the SAIF of the FDIC into a new Deposit Insurance Fund (the&nbsp;&#147;DIF&#148;); (ii)&nbsp;allows the FDIC, after March&nbsp;31,&nbsp;2010, to increase deposit insurance coverage by an adjustment for inflation and requires the &nbsp;FDIC&#146;s Board of Directors, not later than April 1, 2010 and every five years thereafter, to consider whether such an increase is warranted; (iii) increases the deposit insurance limit for certain employee benefit plan deposits from $100,000 to $250,000, subject to adjustments for inflation after March 31, 2010, and provides for pass-through insurance coverage for such deposits; (iv) increases the deposit insurance limit for certain retirement account deposits from $100,000 to $250,000, subject to adjustments for inflation after March 31, 2010; (v) allows the FDIC&#146;s Board of Directors to set deposit insurance premium assessments in any amount the Board of Directors deems necessary or appropriate, after taking into account various factors specified in FDIRA; (vi) replaces the fixed designated reserve ratio of 1.25% with a reserve ratio range of 1.15%-1.50%, with the specific reserve ratio to be determined annually by the FDIC by regulation; (vii) permits the FDIC to revise the risk-based assessment system by regulation; (viii) requires the FDIC, at the end of any year in which the reserve ratio of the DIF exceeds 1.50% of estimated insured deposits, to declare a dividend payable to insured depository institutions in an amount equal to 100% of the amount held by the DIF in excess of the amount necessary to maintain the DIF&#146;s reserve ratio at 1.50% of estimated insured deposits or to declare a dividend equal to 50% of the amount in excess of the amount necessary to maintain the reserve ratio at 1.35% if the reserve ratio is between 1.35%-1.50% of &nbsp;estimated insured deposits; and (ix)&nbsp;provides a one-time credit based upon the assessment base of the institution on December&nbsp;31, 1996 to each insured depository institution that was in existence as of December&nbsp;31, 1996 and paid a deposit insurance assessment prior to that date (or a successor to any such institution).
</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"> The merger of the BIF and the SAIF will take effect no later than July 1, 2006, while the remaining provisions are not effective until the FDIC issues final regulations. &nbsp;FDIRA requires the FDIC to issue final regulations no later than 270 days after enactment: (i) designating a reserve ratio; (ii)&nbsp;implementing increases in deposit insurance coverage; (iii) implementing the dividend requirement; (iv) implementing the one-time assessment credit; and (v) providing for assessments in accordance with FDIRA. &nbsp;
</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>

<P style="page-break-before:always; margin:0pt; padding-left:27pt; text-indent:-28.8pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>DISTRIBUTION OF ASSETS, LIABILITIES AND SHAREHOLDERS' EQUITY;</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:27pt; text-indent:-28.8pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>INTEREST RATES AND</B> <B>INTEREST</B> <B>DIFFERENTIAL</B> (Continued)</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:45pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The following tables set forth the effect of volume and rate changes on interest income and expense for the periods indicated. &nbsp;For purposes of these tables, changes in interest due to volume and rate were determined as follows:</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:28.8pt; text-indent:-28.8pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-13pt; padding-left:45pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Volume Variance </P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-13pt; padding-left:45pt; text-indent:117pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">-</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:45pt; text-indent:135pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">change in volume multiplied by the previous year's rate.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-13pt; padding-left:45pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Rate Variance</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-13pt; padding-left:45pt; text-indent:117pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">-</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:45pt; text-indent:135pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">change in rate multiplied by the previous year's volume.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-13pt; padding-left:180pt; text-indent:-135pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Rate/Volume Variance </P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-13pt; padding-left:180pt; text-indent:-18pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">-</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:180pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">change in volume multiplied by the change in rate. &nbsp;This variance was allocated to volume variance and rate variance in proportion to the relationship of the absolute dollar amount of the change in each.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:28.8pt; text-indent:-28.8pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:28.8pt; text-indent:-28.8pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><BR></P>

<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:12.35pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Concentrations of Credit Risk.</I></B> &nbsp;The Bank grants commercial loans, residential real estate mortgages, and installment loans to individuals mainly in its market area. &nbsp;Commercial loans include loans collateralized by business assets. &nbsp;At December 31, 2005, commercial loans made up approximately 66.3% of the loan portfolio and the loans were expected to be repaid from cash flow from operations of businesses. &nbsp;Commercial loans concentrated in real estate development and investment totaled $21.2 million or 6.3% of total loans at December 31, 2005. &nbsp;Residential real estate mortgage loans made up approximately 28.7% of the loan portfolio and were collateralized by residential real estate. &nbsp;Installment loans to individuals made up approximately 5.0% of the loan portfolio and were primarily collateralized by consumer assets.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>

<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-12pt; padding-left:27pt; padding-right:58.5pt; font-family:Times New Roman">Interest income actually recorded on nonaccrual loans and included in net income for the period</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:27pt; padding-right:58.5pt; text-indent:428.65pt; line-height:11pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><U>-</U></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:27pt; padding-right:58.5pt; text-indent:-27pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-12pt; padding-left:27pt; padding-right:58.5pt; font-family:Times New Roman">Interest income not recognized during the period</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-12pt; padding-left:27pt; padding-right:58.5pt; text-indent:396pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><U>$</U></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:27pt; padding-right:58.5pt; text-indent:428.65pt; line-height:11pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><U>-</U></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:36pt; text-indent:-36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:36pt; text-indent:-36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Discussion of the Nonaccrual Policy.</I></B> &nbsp;The accrual of interest income is discontinued when the collection of a loan or interest, in whole or in part, is doubtful. &nbsp;When interest accruals are discontinued, interest income accrued in the current period is reversed. &nbsp;While loans which are past due 90 days (180 days for residential real estate loans) or more as to interest or principal payments are considered for nonaccrual status, management may elect to continue the accrual of interest when the estimated net realizable value of collateral, in management&#146;s judgment, is sufficient to cover the principal balance and accrued interest.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Potential Problem Loans.</I></B> &nbsp;As of December 31, 2005, there were approximately $15.5 million of loans on the Bank&#146;s internal watch list. &nbsp;Watch list credits are loans that are monitored more closely due to current financial performance of the borrowers or where management has modest concern on the ability of the borrowers to comply with the present loan repayment terms. &nbsp;Management believes that such loans will not materially impact future operating results, liquidity, or capital resources.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:72pt; text-indent:-28.8pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Foreign Outstandings, Loan Concentrations, and Other Interest-Bearing Assets.</I></B> &nbsp;There are no foreign outstandings, loan concentrations, or other interest-bearing assets as of December&nbsp;31, 2005.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:45pt; text-indent:-28.8pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=center><BR></P>
