<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:9pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=justify><B>Overview</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company was formed in February 1996 for the purpose of organizing the Bank. &nbsp;The Company opened the Bank for business in February 1997 with $10.0 million in assets and has grown to approximately $481.1 million as of December 31, 2005. &nbsp;In 2005 and 2004, the Company experienced significant growth as our assets increase d
 $192.8 million over the two years. &nbsp;This growth was partially due to the expansion of the institutional business banking line in conjunction with a strategic decision by management and the board of directors to grow the asset size of the Bank to help manage the increasing cost of operating in a
 highly regulated industry and being a
public company. &nbsp;Management anticipates that, as a result of a modestly growing economy in the Michiana area and the increased asset size of the Company, the growth in assets may continue but likely
at a slower annual percentage rate than experienced historically and compared to the last two years. &nbsp;However, management does expect that the Company&#146;s assets will continue to grow by taking additional market share, expanding into new markets, expanding existing business lines, and as the local economy grows.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank continues to focus on the goals established at its inception. &nbsp;The se goals include offering
 a distinct approach to serving its clients using an operating philosophy that emphasizes integrity in dealing with others, an extraordinary execution of service, and innovation, with technology-driven delivery of products. &nbsp;This approach, along with management&#146;s strong ties to the Indiana and Michigan communities it serves, has been the foundation of its success in building client relationships and maintaining controlled, profitable growth. &nbsp;This focus allows the Bank to operate in an effective and efficient manner providing exceptional client service yet permitting the Bank to be competitive in the marketplace. &nbsp;The operating philosophy is reflected in the results of the Company&#146;s operations and should be considered as they are analyzed. &nbsp;A consistently low overhead ratio continues to allow for competitive product pricing and lower service charges for the Bank&#146;s clients. &nbsp;The focus on credit quality is evident as the Bank has not yet experienced a single loan charge-off, which in turn allows for more efficient use of the Company&#146;s resources, including time, dollars, and energy. &nbsp;These established goals have made the Company unique in the marketplace and unique to its clients. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The return on average assets ( &#147;
ROAA &#148;
) and return on average equity ( &#147;
ROAE &#148;
) steadily increased from the inception of the Company through 2002. &nbsp;
In 2003, the ROAA and ROAE were negatively impacted by severance expense and professional fees related to the departure of two of the Company&#146;s officers. &nbsp;In both
2005 and 2004 ,
 ROAA was 0.75% .
&nbsp;ROAE was 11.84% for 2005 and 9.52% for 2004, returning to levels experienced prior to 2003.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"> S
ince its
 inception the Bank
has focus ed
 on maintaining
superior asset quality. &nbsp;As a general rule, the most credit worthy borrowers are able to negotiate very favorable pricing terms in the market. &nbsp;Additionally, for strategic reasons, the Bank has retained substantially all of its residential mortgage loans. &nbsp; I
t is generally accepted that residential mortgage loans are less risky than other types of loans. &nbsp;Therefore, the interest rates on residential mortgages are less than other types of term loans. &nbsp;These two factors have contributed to the superior asset quality record achieved by the Bank but have also resulted in a more modest net interest margin. &nbsp;Partially offsetting the lower net interest margin is the Company&#146;s low overhead ratio (non-interest expense divided by average assets). &nbsp;In response to clients needs and a flat yield curve, starting in 2006 the Company will begin offering saleable, servicing released, mortgage products in addition to the existing mortgage products to provide clients longer term fixed rate products. &nbsp;Management does not expect these new salable products to have a significant impact on
 the Company&#146;s loan growth but should produce some additional fee income from the gains on the sales of these loans.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Another important aspect of the operating philosophy of the Company is to continue to grow at an appropriate rate resulting in fixed non-interest expense becom ing
 a smaller percentage of total assets over time. &nbsp;Management would expect that this will
lead to continued improvement in the ROAA and ROAE. &nbsp;Non-interest expense as a percentage of total average
assets was 1.42%, 1.56%, and 2.03% for 2005, 2004, and 2003.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">In March of 2005 ,
 the Company sold $5.0 million in trust preferred securities. &nbsp;The proceeds from the sale of the trust preferred securities were used to purchase an equivalent amount of subordinated debentures from the Company. &nbsp;The trust preferred securities carry a fixed rate of 6.27% and have a stated maturity of 30 years, and, in effect, are guaranteed by the Company. &nbsp;The securities are redeemable at par after five
years. &nbsp;The debentures will mature and the capital securities must be redeemed in 2035, although the Company has the option to shorten the maturity date to a date not earlier than March 31, 2010, pending the prior approval of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, if required.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">In April of 2005 ,
 the Company opened its
new banking center in Elkhart, Indiana. &nbsp; Although s
everal new employees were added to staff
 the new banking center ,
 ten of the employees needed to run
an additional full service location came from the Bank&#146;s existing employee base. &nbsp; C
ombined with the
 reasonable overhead cost for the new location , this
 was the reason non-interest expense s
 for the Company were not significantly impacted by this new banking center.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The following discussion provides additional information regarding the Company&#146;s financial condition at December 31, 2005 and 2004 and operations for the years ended December 31, 2005, 2004, and 2003. &nbsp;The objective of this financial review is to enhance the reader&#146;s understanding and should be read in conjunction with the Company&#146;s consolidated financial statements and notes thereto included in this Annual Report on
Form 10-K. &nbsp;For a description of the Company&#146;s material
accounting policies, see Note 1 of Notes to the Consolidated Financial Statements.</P>
<P style="margin-top:5pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Critical Accounting Policies</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Certain of the Company's accounting policies are important to the portrayal of the Company's financial condition, since they require management to make difficult, complex, or subjective judgments, some of which may relate to matters that are inherently uncertain. &nbsp;Estimates associated with these policies are susceptible to material changes as a result of changes in facts and circumstances. &nbsp;Some of the facts and circumstances, which could affect these judgments, include changes in interest rates, the performance of the economy or the financial condition of the Bank&#146;s
borrowers. &nbsp;Management believes that its critical accounting policies include determining the allowance for loan losses.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Allowance for loan losses.</I></B><I> &nbsp;</I>The Company maintains an allowance to absorb probable loan losses inherent in the portfolio. &nbsp;The allowance for loan losses is maintained at a level that management considers to be adequate based on ongoing monthly assessments and evaluations of the collectibility of the Bank&#146;s loans. &nbsp;Provisions for loan losses are based on the management&#146;s review of credit loss experience among its peers and such factors that, in management&#146;s judgment, deserve consideration under existing economic conditions in estimating probable loan losses. &nbsp;Management&#146;s strategy for credit risk management oversight includes a combination of conservative exposure limits significantly below legal lending limits, conservative underwriting standards, and comprehensive loan documentation and review. &nbsp;The strategy also emphasizes diversification at the industry and client levels, regular credit examinations and periodic management reviews of large credit exposures and loans experiencing deterioration of credit quality.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The allowance for loan losses balance and the provision for loan losses are determined by management based upon periodic reviews of the loan portfolio. &nbsp;Through the loan review and credit department, management allocates specific portions of the allowance for loan losses based on specifically identifiable problem loans. &nbsp;In addition, as the Bank does not have an established charge-off history, management consider s
 the level of charge-offs on loans experienced by local peer financial institutions having loan portfolio mix and risk characteristics similar to the Bank&#146;s loan portfolio mix and risk characteristics. &nbsp;Estimating the risk of loss and the amount of loss is necessarily subjective. &nbsp;Accordingly, the allowance is maintained by management at a level considered adequate to cover losses that are currently anticipated based on the combination of the loss experience of the Bank and local peers, general economic conditions, information about specific borrower situations including their financial position, and collateral values and other factors and estimates which are subject to change over time. &nbsp;The Reserve Analysis is reviewed regularly by management and the board of directors.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">General allocations for loan types may be adjusted for significant factors that, in management&#146;s judgment, reflect the impact of any current conditions on loss recognition. &nbsp;Factors that management considers in the analysis include the effects of the national and local economies, trends in the nature and volume of loans, changes in mix, asset quality trends, loan grades, risk management and loan administration, changes in the internal lending policies and credit standards, examination results from bank regulatory agencies ,
 and the Bank&#146;s internal independent loan reviews.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">An unallocated reserve is maintained to recognize the imprecision in estimating and measuring loss when evaluating reserves for individual loans or pools of loans. &nbsp;Reserves on individual loans and general allocation rates are reviewed quarterly and adjusted as necessary based on changing borrower and/or collateral conditions.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">While management's periodic analysis of the adequacy of the allowance for loan losses may allocate portions of the allowance for specific problem loan situations, the entire allowance is available for any loan charge-offs that occur.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company has not substantively changed its overall approach in the determination of the allowance for loan losses. &nbsp;As the Company has matured and economic conditions have changed, the Company has made only minor changes in assumptions or estimation techniques as compared to prior periods that impacted the determination of the December 31, 2005 allowance for loan and lease losses. &nbsp;The Bank has not experienced any charge-offs from loans receivable since inception and at December 31, 2005, no portion of the allowance for loan losses was allocated to impaired loan balances, as there were no loans considered impaired.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Based on the policy discussed above and further discussion found in the provision for loan losses analysis in the Results of Operations section of this document, management is of the opinion that the allowance of $3.6 million was adequate to absorb estimated loan losses associated with the loan portfolio at December 31, 2005. &nbsp;However, there can be no assurance that future losses will not exceed the estimated amounts or that the Company will not be required to make additional provision for losses in the future.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>FINANCIAL CONDITION</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>December 31, 2005 Compared with December 31, 2004</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">During 2005, the Company&#146;s total
assets increased from $399.1 million on December 31, 2004, to $481.1 million on December 31, 2005. &nbsp;This was a 20.6% increase compared to a 38.4% increase during the previous year. &nbsp;A consistent emphasis on deposit growth ,
 along with other borrowing sources ,
 provided the funding needed to increase the net loan portfolio by $56.7 million and increase the securities portfolio by $25.4 million during 2005. &nbsp;These two areas of growth were the primary contributors to the $82.0 million overall increase in total
assets.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">At December 31, 2005, cash and cash equivalents decreased $3.5 million, from $45.3 million in 2004 to $41.8 million in 2005. &nbsp;In the fourth quarter of 2005, property tax dollars were collected as scheduled and the proceeds were used to replenish our municipal clients &#146;
 deposit balances. &nbsp;Different th a
n in 2004, property tax dollars were collected and paid to municipalities twice during 2005 therefore reducing the level of total municipal deposits at the end of the year and
resulting in a decrease in the overall balance in cash and cash equivalents. &nbsp;In 2004 ,
 there was a delay in the receipt of property tax dollars from taxpayers used to fund Indiana municipalities ,
 therefore reducing to below the
normal levels of
 our municipal clients &#146;
 account balances during the second half of the year. &nbsp;Since the entire 2004 property tax collection was completed in December of that year ,
the deposits from our municipal clients were greater than what was experienced in 2005. &nbsp;Due to the timing of the receipts of these deposits, on the last business day of the year approximately $30.0 million in 2005 compared to $25.0 million in 2004 was deposited by municipalities , which
 significantly increased the Company&#146;s year-end balances of cash and cash equivalents. &nbsp;The average balances during 2005 and 2004 for cash and cash equivalents were $23.0 million and $23.9 million. &nbsp;These average balances represent ed
 more normal levels of cash and cash equivalents. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The $25.4 million increase in securities available for sale, from $58.2 million at December 31, 2004 to $83.6 million at December 31, 2005, was a significant change in the Company&#146;s investment portfolio
 during the year. &nbsp;The growth experienced in securities available for sale was primarily in U.S. government and federal agency securities, from $37.9 million at December 31, 2004 to $57.4 million at December 31, 2005, and obligations of states and political subdivision securities, from $14.7 million at December 31, 2004 to $20.3 million at December 31, 2005. &nbsp;The increase in securities available for sale was primarily the desire
 to deploy funds from the growth in deposits during the first quarter of 2005.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company&#146;s loan portfolio increased $56.7 million or 20.4%, from $278.4 million at December 31, 2004 to $335.1 million at December 31, 2005. &nbsp;This growth was primarily in the Company&#146;s commercial loan portfolio, which increased $31.6 million or 55.8% of the total
2005 annual increase. &nbsp;The residential mortgage portfolio had an increase of $17.5 million or 30.9% of the 2005 annual increase. &nbsp;The loan growth during the year was
attribut able
 to adding new loan relationships, the expansion of borrowing relationships from existing clients, and from the purchase of loan participations.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The continued significant concentration of the loan portfolio in commercial loans and the continued future growth of this portion of the Bank&#146;s lending business are consistent with the Company&#146;s stated strategy of focusing on small- to mid-size businesses. &nbsp;Commercial loans make up 66.3% of net loans at December 31, 2005 and approximately 74.5% of the commercial loans are secured by real estate. &nbsp;Management anticipates this concentration in commercial loans will continue as additional relationships are developed as a result of current calling efforts, the utilization of newer lending personnel, and the expansion into a new market area. &nbsp;The deposit accounts related to the Bank&#146;s commercial lending business generate a significant amount of local deposits, and are an important funding source for the Bank.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The quality of the loan portfolio remains strong. &nbsp;The Company has not experienced any charge-offs since inception. &nbsp;Management believes it has
 instilled a very strong credit culture within the lending department as it pertains to the underwriting and administration processes and no change is expected as the loan portfolio grows. &nbsp;Over 91.8% of the loan portfolio consists of loans extended directly to companies and individuals doing business and residing within the Company&#146;s market area or having strong, long-standing ties to management. &nbsp;The remaining portion is comprised of participations in
commercial loans with non-affiliated commercial banks outside of the immediate area, which are underwritten using the same loan underwriting criteria as though the Bank was the originating bank.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The economy in the Company&#146;s market area began a steady but modest improvement
in 2003 and has continued improving during 2005. &nbsp;During this period, the Company has experienced growth without a
significant change in asset quality or the need to record any non-performing loans. &nbsp;However, due to the modest rate of the growth in the current economy and normal business cycles, borrowers may experience difficulty, and the level of non-performing loans, charge-offs, and delinquencies could rise and require further increases in the provision for loan losses.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Premise s
 and equipment increased from $2.4 million at December 31, 2004 to $3.3 million at December 31, 2005. &nbsp;This increase, which occurred
primarily in the first quarter of 2005, was due to the completion of the Company&#146;s new Elkhart banking center. &nbsp;As part of the Company&#146;s expansion into Elkhart, Indiana, the Company became a 49.0% owner in a limited liability corporation, Riverfront Partners, LLC (&#147;LLC&#148;), in part
to help ensure that the Company had a level of control over its new location. &nbsp;The other owners, a local development group, own 51.0% of the new location with
 responsibl ity
 for the construction of the building and the continued management of the property. &nbsp;Due to the nature of the LLC ,
 the accounting guidance classified it as a variable interest entity requiring it to be consolidated into the Company&#146;s financial statements. &nbsp;The Company&#146;s Elkhart banking center occupies the first floor of a three-story facility and the impact to the Company&#146;s net income is the profit or loss in the LLC and the allocated expense for the portion of the building the Company occupies. &nbsp;The net cost of this new location has not and is not expected to significantly impact the Company&#146;s financial statements. &nbsp;The total cost of the building was approximately $1.7 million and is consolidated in the Company&#146;s financial statements.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The $30.7 million growth in deposits in 2005 was mainly in interest-bearing deposits. &nbsp;This was a 9.8% increase in 2005 compared to a 50.8% increase in 2004. &nbsp;Interest-bearing deposits made up approximately 82.6% of the Bank&#146;s deposits at December 31, 2005. &nbsp;As discussed above in cash and cash equivalents, deposits of approximately $30.0 million were made on the last business day of 2005, and therefore classified as non-interest bearing. &nbsp;On the first business day of 2006, the funds became interest bearing when collected
. &nbsp;The average balance of interest bearing deposits was $297.1 million during 2005 and $193.1 million during 2004. &nbsp;Interest bearing deposit balances were $283.5 million at December 31, 2005 and $256.5 million at December 31, 2004. &nbsp;The $104.0 million increase in the average balance of interest bearing deposits from 2004 to 2005, compared to the $27.0 million increase in year-over-year interest bearing deposits at December 31, demonstrates that the growth in interest bearing deposits came later in 2004 and decreased slightly from their highest balance during 2005. &nbsp;These results were primarily due to the Company&#146;s growth in new and existing deposit relationships with municipalities. &nbsp;The Bank expects to continue to see additional growth in deposits during 2006 and to retain a significant portion of the deposits that existed at December 31, 2005.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company&#146;s municipal business line provides a significant percentage of the Company&#146;s deposit funding and also contributes to the fluctuation in the Company&#146;s short term funding balances as the municipal account balances increase and decrease. &nbsp;At December 31, 2005, $143.2 million or 41.7% of the $343.4 million of deposits were from the Company&#146;s municipal business line. &nbsp;The typical funding pattern by which the municipalities operate is dependent on the collection of property tax dollars by the local governments. &nbsp;Most of the property taxes are collected in May and November by the local governments and the funding to the municipalities is provided in June and December. &nbsp;As the funds are used by the municipalities ,
 their account balances decrease until the next funding date. &nbsp;As these municipal balances fluctuate, the Company uses similar cost short-term funding sources to manage the funding needs.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">As the municipal business line grows ,
 the Company expects that its municipal government clients will increase their use of corporate agency trust agreements. &nbsp;Such agreements
 provide municipalities with another option for managing their accounts by allowing a portion of their deposited funds to be swept into their trust accounts. &nbsp;Since these trust accounts are not recorded on
the Bank&#146;s balance sheet ,
 they do not require an allocation of the Bank&#146;s capital. &nbsp;As additional funding is required at the Bank, the funds in the trust accounts can be swept back to their accounts at the Bank. &nbsp;With the utilization of these trust relationships ,
 &nbsp;the fluctuation in their deposit accounts at the Bank due to their funding patterns can partially be managed without the use of short-term funding sources. &nbsp;Depending on the asset growth of the Company ,
 the ability to utilize the Bank&#146;s Trust Department as another option for the municipal clients will vary. &nbsp;The growth of this business line continues to be a focus for the Company and it is expected to continue to be a significant funding source for the Company. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">To date, the Bank has been successful in attracting and retaining client relationships, which have provided a significant portion of the funding needed to grow the loan portfolio and increase the investment portfolio. &nbsp;Management has accepted $40.6 million in brokered certificates of deposit as of December 31, 2005 compared to $50.7 million at December 31, 2004. &nbsp;Management utilizes this funding source to assist in the overall asset/liability management of the Bank. &nbsp;For the most part, these certificates of deposit are longer term
 and are used to assist the bank in funding term loans and as a means to manage the Company&#146;s overall interest rate risk.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Federal funds purchased increased to $28.6 million at December 31, 2005 compared to $2.8 million of federal funds purchased at December 31, 2004. &nbsp;Federal funds purchased are used as a temporary funding source by the Bank to meet short-term liquidity needs. &nbsp;The Bank has over $48.5 million in federal funds lines established at correspondent banks. &nbsp;At times, there will be differences between asset growth and the funding that supports this growth. &nbsp;The use of federal funds purchased provides short-term funding for these differences when funding is needed.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Federal Home Loan Bank of Indianapolis (&#147;FHLB&#148;) advances increased $18.5 million, or 44.4%, to $60.2 million as of December 31, 2005 compared to $41.7 million at December 31, 2004. &nbsp;Management uses FHLB advances primarily to assist in the overall asset and liability management of the Bank and for general liquidity purposes. &nbsp;Due to the significant deposits expected from the Company&#146;s municipal clients in the fourth quarter of 2005 and the first quarter of 2006, $28.2 million of the $60.2 million of advances at December 31, 2005 had an overnight term. &nbsp;With the collected deposit funds available during the first week of 2006 ,
 the $28.2 million of overnight advances were repaid leaving $32.0 million of FHLB advances that are longer term
. &nbsp;As of December 31, 2005, the Company held $3.2 million of FHLB stock.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Securities sold under agreement to repurchase decreased by $1.7 million at December 31, 2005 compared to the prior year. &nbsp;As part of the Company&#146;s sweep accounts, collected funds from business clients&#146; noninterest-bearing checking accounts are invested into overnight interest-bearing repurchase agreements. &nbsp;Although not considered a deposit account and therefore not FDIC insured, the repurchase agreements from local corporate clients have historically been a very stable source of funding for the Bank.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Management believes the ability to attract and retain deposits is attributed to more personalized service than the competition. &nbsp;The deposit growth in 2005, along with the use of $25.8 million of federal funds purchased, $18.5 million of FHLB advances, and a
$2.4 million increase in equity achieved primarily through the retention of earnings, provided the funding needed to manage and support
the $82.0 million growth in assets.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:13.4pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>RESULTS OF OPERATIONS</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>2005 Compared with 2004</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Overview. &nbsp;</I></B>Consolidated net income for the year ended December 31, 2005 was $3.2 million compared to $2.4 million in 2004 for an increase of $811,000 or 33.7%. &nbsp;Income per common share for 2005 increased to $1.85 basic and $1.73 fully-diluted, compared to $1.39 basic and $1.33 fully-diluted in 2004. &nbsp;The exercise of stock options, which increased the average number of shares outstanding by approximately 11,000 shares as compared to 2004, reduced the 33.7% increase in net income to a 33.1% increase in basic income per common share. &nbsp;The increase in net income for 2005 compared to
the prior year was comprised of an increase in net interest income of $766,000, a decrease in provision for loan losses of $261,000, an increase in non-interest income of $227,000, and a decrease of &nbsp;$181,000 in income tax expense offset by an increase in non-interest expense of $624,000.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Net Interest Income</I></B>. &nbsp;The largest component of net income is net interest income. &nbsp;Net interest income is the difference between interest income, principally from loans and securities available for sale, and interest expense, principally on customer deposits and borrowings. &nbsp;Changes in the net interest income are the result of changes in volume, net interest spread, and net interest margin. &nbsp;Volume refers to the average dollar levels of interest-earning assets and interest-bearing liabilities. &nbsp;Net interest spread refers to the difference between the average yield on interest-earning assets and the average cost of interest-bearing liabilities. &nbsp;Net interest margin refers to net interest income divided by average interest-earning assets and is influenced by the level and relative mix of interest-earning assets and interest-bearing liabilities.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Net interest income during 2005 was $10.2 million, an increase of $776,000, or 8.1%, over the $9.4 million earned in 2004. &nbsp;The yield on the Company&#146;s interest-earning assets increased by 55 basis points to 5.37% during 2005, from 4.82% in 2004. &nbsp;This increase was primarily the result of increased volume in interest-earning assets due to growth in both securities available for sale and loans receivable combined with higher interest rates received during 2005. &nbsp;Rates paid on the Company&#146;s interest-bearing liabilities increased 114 basis points to 3.16% during 2005, from 2.02% in 2004. &nbsp;This increase was primarily the result of higher interest rates paid during 2005 combined with increased volume in interest-bearing liabilities due to increased deposits and borrowings. &nbsp;The greater increase in the rates paid on the Company&#146;s interest-bearing liabilities compared to the yields received on the Company&#146;s interest-earning assets caused the net interest margin to decline 55 basis points from 3.08% at December 31, 2004 to 2.53% at December 31, 2005. &nbsp; Even with t
he declining margin during 2005 the Company
generated $766,000 more net interest income than the previous year due to the volume variance from the growth in interest-earning assets being greater than the negative rate variance from higher rates paid on interest-bearing liabilities.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The increase in the short-term interest rates during 2005 combined with a slower increase in long-term interest rates has flattened the yield curve. &nbsp;This flattening has impacted the cost of the Company&#146;s interest bearing liabilities more significantly than the yields available on the Company&#146;s interest earning assets, resulting in the compression of the net interest margin. &nbsp;The interest bearing liabilities were partially impacted due to the increase in short-term interest rates during 2005 combined with the growth in the municipal business line. The rates paid to a significant portion of the Company&#146;s municipal accounts are indexed to the short-term rates and adjust daily which has contributed to the higher interest expense.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Management believes that the current level of interest rates is significantly driven by external factors and that the net interest margin could continue to compress if a flat or inverted yield curve continues. &nbsp;As the Company grows, the additional leveraging of capital increases the percentage of interest-bearing liabilities used to fund the asset growth and can also push the net interest margin lower. &nbsp;If long-term interest rates continue to increase at a slow pace, net interest income can be increased by growth in interest-earning assets and interest-bearing liabilities. &nbsp;Management expects short-term interest rates to continue to increase during the first quarter of 2006. &nbsp;Therefore, the Company will continue its effort to optimize its asset mix, and thereby seek to help neutralize the net interest margin impact associated with a changing rate environment and continue to add volume to the balance sheet to manage the net interest income in the future. &nbsp; However
, if short-term interest rates stabilize or begin to decrease or the yield curve begins to generate more historic interest rate spreads, net interest income can be increased by not only the growth in interest-earning assets, but by an expected increase in the net interest margin based on the structure of the Company&#146;s interest bearing liabilit ies
.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Provision for Loan Losses</I></B>. &nbsp;There was no provision added to the allowance for loan losses during 2005. &nbsp;This reflected
a decrease from $261,000 expensed during the same periods in 2004. &nbsp;Management&#146;s decision not to record any provision expense for the year ended 2005 was attributed to the credit quality that the Company has experienced since its inception combined with the result of management&#146;s loan loss reserve analysis, which is used in determining the amount of allowance for loan loss required for the loan portfolio. &nbsp;The additional weighting of the Company&#146;s own loan loss experience in the reserve analysis also has contributed to the decision to decrease the allowance for loan loss as a percentage of gross loans. &nbsp;The description of our reserve analysis,
along with other relevant allowance for loan loss details ,
 is included in the Critical Account ing
 Policies section above
. &nbsp;The allowance for loan losses as a percentage of total loans outstanding as of December 31, 2005 was 1.06% compared to 1.27% at December 31, 2004. &nbsp;If the Company&#146;s continued loss experience is similar to the past and the local economy remains stable, it is expected that our reserve analysis may
continue to require a smaller allowance for loan loss as a percentage of total loans outstanding. &nbsp;Depending on the results of
the reserve analysis
and the pace of loan growth in the future, the provision to the allowance for loan loss required may not be consistent with the levels experienced in prior years
.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company
has not experienced any charge-offs from loans receivable since inception. &nbsp;At December 31, 2005, no portion of the allowance for loan losses was allocated to impaired loan balances, as there were no loans considered impaired. &nbsp;Management believes the allowance for loan losses at December 31, 2005 was adequate to absorb losses in the loan portfolio, including probable incurred losses due to the current state of the economy. &nbsp;However, there can be no assurance that future losses will not exceed the estimated amounts or that the Company
will not be required to make additional provision for losses in the future.</P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Non-interest Income</I></B>. &nbsp;Non-interest income increased by $227,000 during 2005 to $956,000 compared to $729,000 during 2004. &nbsp;Service charges on deposit accounts decreased slightly during the year
as more clients maintained higher average balances in their demand deposit accounts to pay for service charges. &nbsp;Due to the Company&#146;s operating philosophy, which includes a general aversion to excessive service charges on deposit accounts, significant changes in service charges would not be expected except as a result of continued growth in deposit relationships.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Gain on sale of securities during 2005 was $11,000 compared to $106,000 in 2004. &nbsp;The Company primarily sells securities for liquidity purposes that may result in security gains or losses. &nbsp;The sale of available for sale securities during 2005 was to provide liquidity to help fund the growth in the loan portfolio. &nbsp;Except for liquidity needs, the Company&#146;s intent is to hold their investment securities to maturity. </P>
<P style="margin-top:4.6pt; margin-bottom:4.6pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The addition of bank owned life insurance (&#147;BOLI&#148;)
has been the most significant increase to non-interest income. &nbsp; BOLI
 produced $363,000 of non-interest income during 2005 compared to $72,000 in 2004 due to the increase in the cash surrender value of life insurance held by the Bank
. &nbsp;Management believes that the earnings on life insurance will be maintained similar to
 the level of earnings experienced in 2005. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Non-interest Expense</I></B>. &nbsp;The main components of non-interest expense were salaries and employee benefits, occupancy and equipment, professional fees, and data processing. &nbsp;Non-interest expense for 2005 was $6.8 million as compared to $6.2 million in 2004, an increase of $624,000 or 10.0%. &nbsp;While overhead continues to increase in order to support a larger client base and overall size, management continues to be focused on controlling these expenses without impairing the quality of service provided to clients or the ability to grow the Company. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Salaries and employee benefits, which was
the largest component of non-interest expense, experienced the most significant dollar change of any non-interest expense component. &nbsp;For 2005, total salaries and employee benefits were $4.7 million compared to $4.3 million in 2004. &nbsp;The increase in employee salaries and benefits was a result of merit increases, increases in the cost of employee benefits, and the hiring of eighteen new employees since December 31, 2004. &nbsp;Management believes that these increased costs are necessary to continue to grow the business and
 to provide high quality client service. &nbsp;Some additional hiring is expected during 2006 to continue to meet the needs of the Company as it grows ,
 although the level of hiring
is not expected to be as significant as in 2005.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Occupancy and equipment expense increased $126,000 for 2005 compared to 2004. &nbsp;The Company has experienced an increase in occupancy and equipment expense due to the addition of the Elkhart banking center for the last
nine months of 2005 and the additional investing in technology that occurred
 during the year. &nbsp;Professional fees decreased $68,000 for 2005 compared to 2004. &nbsp;This decrease was due to a reduction in required legal services during 2005 and the hiring of an internal auditor that reduced permanently the Company&#146;s outsourced internal audit costs. &nbsp;Data processing increased $36,000 for 2005 from increased data processing activity due to our larger
 c lient
base.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Monitoring and controlling non-interest costs, while at the same time providing high quality service to clients, is one of management&#146;s top priorities. &nbsp;Management does anticipate that in 2006 these costs will increase somewhat
as the result of growth ,
 although
 these costs will be carefully monitored and are not expected to have a significant impact on net income. &nbsp;The efficiency ratio for the Company for 2005 was 61.3 % compared to 61.2% for 2004.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Income Taxes. &nbsp;</I></B>Income tax expense was $1.1 million during 2005 compared to $1.3 million in 2004. &nbsp;The effective tax rates were 25.4% and 34.7% for 2005 and 2004. The decrease in the effective tax rate for 2005 was, among other items, the result of an increase in the tax exempt interest income from additional purchases of municipal securities, tax exempt non-interest income from bank owned life insurance, and the transfer of most all of the investment securities portfolio into the Bank&#146;s Delaware investment subsidiary reducing the state tax liability from the interest income received at the subsidiary. &nbsp;Management anticipates that these recently implemented tax planning strategies will help maintain the effective tax rate at approximately the same level as experienced in 2005.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>2004 Compared with 2003</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Overview. &nbsp;</I></B>Consolidated net income for 2004, was $2.4 million compared to $1.8 million in 2003 for an increase of $584,000 or 32.0%. &nbsp;Net income for 2003 was negatively impacted by approximately $184,000, net of tax, from severance expenses during the third quarter of 2003 related to the departure of two officers from the Company. &nbsp;Income per common share for 2004 increased to $1.39 basic and $1.33 fully-diluted, compared to $1.08 basic and $1.05 fully-diluted in 2003. &nbsp;The exercise of stock options, which increased the average number of shares outstanding by approximately 50,295 shares as compared to 2003, reduced the 32.0% increase in net income to a 28.7% increase in basic income per common share. &nbsp;The increase in net income for 2004 compared to the prior year was comprised of an increase in net interest income of $1.1 million and a decrease in provision for loan losses of $66,000 offset by an increase in non-interest expense of $363,000 and an increase in income tax expense of $236,000.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Net Interest Income</I></B>. &nbsp;Net interest income during 2004 was $9.4 million, an increase of $1.1 million, or 13.3%, over the $8.3 million earned in 2003. &nbsp;Yields on the Company&#146;s interest-earning assets declined by 21 basis points to 4.82% during 2004, from 5.03% in 2003. &nbsp;This decrease was the result of the change in the mix of assets due to the significant growth in deposits during the first quarter of 2004 along with a lower rate environment at the beginning of 2004 as compared to the beginning of 2003. &nbsp;Rates paid on interest-bearing liabilities remained at 2.02% for both 2004 and 2003. &nbsp;Due to a decline in the yield on the Company&#146;s interest-earning assets with no change in the rate paid on the Company&#146;s interest-bearing liabilities, coupled with the growth in interest earning assets, the net interest margin declined 22 basis points from 3.30% at December 31, 2003 to 3.08% at December 31, 2004. &nbsp;A higher growth rate in interest-earning assets and interest bearing liabilities can generate more net interest income during a period despite a decreasing margin, as long as the volume variance is greater than the negative rate variance. &nbsp;Also, as demonstrated during the first quarter of 2004, a significant change in the mix of interest earning assets or interest bearing liabilities can impact the net interest margin. For 2004, the increase in net interest income of $1.1 million over 2003 was primarily the result of increased volume.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">At the end of December 2003, the property tax dollars were collected and began to replenish the municipal deposit accounts, which provided substantial amounts of cash and cash equivalents to the Company. &nbsp;Since the Bank&#146;s municipal clients borrowed funds during the period, a portion of the funds deposited at the end of 2003 and January 2004 was used to pay back the borrowings and was only deposited for a short time. &nbsp;As the municipal deposit balances returned to more normal levels, the Company was able to make the shift from cash and cash equivalents to securities available for sale and loans that are higher yielding assets.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">During 2004, property tax bills were again delayed. &nbsp;Both the May and September 2004 tax bills were mailed in October 2004, which resulted in the receipt of all property tax dollars for 2004 being collected in November 2004 and paid to the municipalities in December 2004 and January 2005. &nbsp;In anticipation of the influx of deposits in the fourth quarter, management developed a plan to make the necessary balance sheet allocations to help minimize the impact to the net interest margin in the first quarter of 2005.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The events described above impacted the Company&#146;s net interest margin negatively during the first quarter of 2004 due to the lower spread on the short-term assets while the Company held the funds and from the temporarily inflated cash and cash equivalent balances that increased the average interest earning asset balance for the period. &nbsp;The excess cash and cash equivalent balances were invested in available for sale securities and loans along with the use of additional deposit funding during the second, third, and fourth quarters. &nbsp;The Company&#146;s net interest margin returned to more normal levels but began to drop slightly in the fourth quarter due to more cash in the asset mix again.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">As discussed above, the lower margin for 2004, was significantly impacted by the increase in cash and cash equivalents from the municipal funds deposited at the beginning and end of 2004. &nbsp;The margin did rebound from the lower level during the first three months of 2004, due partially to the asset mix variance.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Provision for Loan Losses</I></B>. &nbsp;Provisions to the allowance for loan losses during 2004 totaled $261,000, a decrease from $327,000 expensed in 2003. &nbsp;The provision expensed for 2004 was slightly lower than the provision expensed in 2003 even with the loan volume being greater in 2004. &nbsp;The decrease in provision expense was the result of management&#146;s Loan Loss Reserve Analysis (&quot;Reserve Analysis&quot;) determining that the amount of allowance for loan loss required for the loan portfolio should decrease as a percentage of gross loans. &nbsp;The allowance for loan losses as a percentage of total loans outstanding as of December 31, 2004 was 1.27% compared to 1.47% at December 31, 2003.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Non-interest Income</I></B>. &nbsp;Non-interest income remained primarily the same at $729,000 for 2004 compared to $733,000 in 2003. &nbsp;Service charges on deposit accounts increased only slightly during the year
as more clients maintained higher average balances in their demand deposit accounts to pay for service charges. &nbsp;Due to the Company&#146;s operating philosophy, which includes a general aversion to excessive service charges on deposit accounts, significant changes in service charges would not be expected except as a result of continued growth in deposit relationships.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Gain on sale of securities in 2004 was $106,000 compared to $236,000 in 2003. &nbsp;The Company primarily sells securities for liquidity purposes that may result in security gains or losses. &nbsp;Except for liquidity needs, the Company&#146;s intent is to hold investment securities to maturity. </P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The purchase of bank owned life insurance added $72,000 of non-interest income in 2004. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Non-interest Expense</I></B>. &nbsp;The main components of non-interest expense were salaries and employee benefits, occupancy and equipment, professional fees, and data processing. &nbsp;Non-interest expense for 2004 was $6.2 million as compared to $5.9 million in 2003, an increase of $363,000 or 6.2%. &nbsp;While overhead continues to increase in order to support its larger client base and overall size, management continues to be focused on controlling these expenses without impairing the quality of service provided to clients or the ability to grow the Company. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Salaries and employee benefits, which were the largest component of non-interest expense, experienced the most significant dollar changes of any non-interest expense component. &nbsp;For 2004, total salaries and employee benefits were $4.3 million compared to $4.0 million in 2003. &nbsp;The increase in employee salaries and benefits for the year was the result of merit increases and the hiring of nine new employees since December 31, 2003.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Occupancy and equipment, professional fees, data processing and other expense did not have any significant changes during 2004 as compared to 2003. &nbsp;Monitoring and controlling non-interest costs, while at the same time providing high quality service to clients, is one of management&#146;s top priorities.</P>
<P style="margin-top:3.8pt; margin-bottom:3.8pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><B><I>Income Taxes. &nbsp;</I></B>Income tax expense was $1.3 million during 2004 compared to $1.0 million in 2003. &nbsp;The effective tax rates were 34.7% and 36.4% for 2004 and 2003. The decrease in the effective tax rate for 2004 was the result of
an increase in the tax exempt interest income from additional purchases of municipal securities, tax exempt non-interest income from the bank owned life insurance, and the transfer of a portion of the investment securities portfolio to the Bank&#146;s Delaware investment subsidiary reducing the state tax liability from the interest income received at the subsidiary.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Liquidity</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt">The Bank&#146;s liquidity is measured by the ability to raise funds through deposits, borrowed funds, capital or cash flow from the repayment or maturities of loans and securities and net profits. &nbsp;These monies can be used to fund loan requests, meet deposit withdrawals, maintain reserve requirements, and support operations. &nbsp;Liquidity is primarily achieved through the growth of deposits (both local and out-of-area) and by maintaining liquid assets such as securities available for sale, matured securities, and federal funds sold. &nbsp;Asset and liability management is the process of managing the balance sheet to achieve a mix of earning assets and liabilities in such a way that manages interest rate risk and assists in achieving an acceptable level of profitability. &nbsp;An important part of the overall asset and liability management process is providing adequate liquidity. &nbsp;Liquid assets were $125.4 million at December 31, 2005 compared to $103.6 million at December 31, 2004 and $59.6 million at December 31, 2003.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">&nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank&#146;s liquidity strategy is to fund loan growth with deposits, repurchase agreements, and FHLB advances and to maintain an adequate level of short- and medium-term investments to meet typical daily loan and deposit activity. &nbsp;Although deposit and repurchase agreement growth from depositors located in the market area have consistently increased, the growth has not been sufficient at times to meet the substantial loan growth and provide monies for additional investing activities. &nbsp;To assist in providing the additional needed funds, the Bank has obtained certificates of deposit from customers outside of the market area and placed by deposit brokers for a fee, as well as certificates of deposit obtained from the deposit owners directly. &nbsp;As of December 31, 2005, brokered deposits totaled $40.6 million, or 11.5% of combined deposits and repurchase agreements, compared to $50.7 million, or 15.7% of combined deposits and repurchase agreements, as of December 31, 2004. &nbsp;The acceptance of brokered deposits is expected to be an ongoing activity due to planned future growth and managing a consistent level of deposits.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:-86.4pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">&nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Bank has the ability to borrow money on a daily basis through correspondent banks via established federal funds purchased lines; however, this is viewed as only a secondary and temporary source of funds. &nbsp;The average balance of federal funds <U>sold</U> during 2005 equaled $1.0 million, compared to $9.5 million average balance of federal funds <U>purchased</U>.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">As a member of the FHLB, the Bank has access to the FHLB's borrowing programs. &nbsp;Based on available collateral at February 28, 2006, the Bank could borrow up to approximately $112.5 million subject to the purchase of additional stock in the FHLB.<FONT COLOR=#FF0000> &nbsp;</FONT>As of December 31, 2005, $60.2 million in FHLB advances was outstanding. &nbsp;The availability could continue to increase if the loan and investment portfolios grow in the future. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">In addition to typical loan funding and deposit flows, the Bank must maintain liquidity to meet the demands of certain unfunded loan commitments and standby letters of credit. &nbsp;As of December 31, 2005, there were a total of $91.7 million in unfunded loan commitments and $2.9 million in unfunded standby letters of credit. &nbsp;Fluctuations in loan balances and commitment levels are monitored, with such data being used in managing overall liquidity.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The statements of cash flows for the periods presented provide an indication of the Company&#146;s sources and uses of cash as well as an indication of its ability to maintain an adequate level of liquidity. &nbsp;A discussion of the statements of cash flows for 2005, 2004, and 2003 follows.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">During all periods presented, the Company experienced a net increase in cash from operating activities. &nbsp;Net cash from operating activities was $2.9 million during 2005 compared to $2.9 million during 2004 and $2.5 million during 2003. &nbsp;The increase in cash from operating activities was primarily a result of the Company&#146;s ability to generate net income of $3.2 million in 2005, $2.4 million in 2004 and $1.8 million in 2003.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">For 2005, 2004, and 2003, the Company experienced a net decrease in net cash from investing activities. &nbsp;Net cash from investing activities was $(84.7) million, $(100.3) million and $(20.7) million for 2005, 2004, and 2003. &nbsp;The changes in net cash from investing activities include purchases, sales, maturities, and calls of securities available for sale, growth in loans receivable, purchase of life insurance, and purchases of premises and equipment.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Net cash flow from financing activities was $78.2 million, $107.3 million, and $32.7 million for 2005, 2004, and 2003. &nbsp;In 2005, the increase was primarily attributable to the growth in deposits of $30.5 million, an increase in federal funds purchased of $25.8 million, an increase in net FHLB advances of $18.5 million, and subordinated debentures of $5.0 million. &nbsp;In 2004, the increase was primarily attributable to the growth in deposits of $105.4 million, an increase of $3.8 million in securities sold under the agreement to repurchase, and an increase in federal funds purchased of $2.8 million offset by a decrease in net FHLB advances of $4.8 million. &nbsp;In 2003, the increase was primarily attributable to the growth in deposits of $29.6 million, net FHLB advances of $3.5 million, and subordinated debentures of $3.0 million offset by a decrease of $3.8 million for securities sold under the agreement to repurchase.</P>

<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=justify><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt" align=justify><B>Capital Resources </B></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt">Retained earnings at December 31, 2005 was $10.0 million compared to $7.2 million at December 31, 2004. &nbsp;The increase in retained earnings was due to the Company&#146;s net income of $3.2 million for 2005 ,
 offset slightly by cash dividends paid of $349,000.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Accumulated other comprehensive income, net of tax from net unrealized loss on securities available for sale, was $928,000 as of December 31, 2005 as compared to $87,000 net unrealized loss on securities available for sale as of December 31, 2004. &nbsp;The decline since December 31, 2004 was primarily attributable to the overall interest rate environment and no permanent impairment in any individual security has been identified as of December 31, 2005. &nbsp;</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:9.15pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:12.45pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Total shareholders&#146; equity was $28.9 million as of December 31, 2005, an increase of $2.4 million from $26.5 million as of December&nbsp;31, 2004. &nbsp;The increase resulted from the increase in the net income for the year as well as the proceeds from the issuance of common stock upon the exercise of stock options decreased by the cash dividends paid by the Company and the unrealized loss on securities available for sale.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:9.15pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:12.45pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company is subject to regulatory capital requirements primarily administered by federal banking regulatory agencies. &nbsp;Failure to meet the various capital requirements can cause regulatory action that could have a direct material effect on the financial statements. &nbsp;Since the Bank commenced operations, both the Company and the Bank have been categorized as w
ell -capitalized
, the highest classification contained within the banking regulations.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:9.15pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:12.45pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The components of total risk-based capital are Tier 1 capital and Tier 2 capital. &nbsp;Tier 1 capital is total shareholders&#146; equity less intangible assets. &nbsp;Tier 2 capital is Tier 1 capital plus a portion of the allowance for loan losses. &nbsp;The allowance for loan losses is includable in Tier 2 capital up to a maximum of 1.25% of risk-weighted assets. &nbsp;The net unrealized appreciation (depreciation) on securities available for sale, net of tax, is not considered in meeting regulatory capital requirements. &nbsp;The tables in Note 12 of the notes to consolidated financial statements provide the minimum regulatory capital requirements and the actual capital ratios at December&nbsp;31, 2005 and 2004.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">As of December 31, 2005, management was not aware of any current recommendations by the banking regulatory authorities which, if they were to be implemented, would have, or are reasonably likely to have, a material adverse effect on the Company&#146;s liquidity, capital resources or operations.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">On March 17, 2005, the Company issued an additional $5.0 million of trust preferred securities to help ensure that the Company and the Bank are well capitalized. &nbsp;The Company and Bank are committed to maintaining a well-capitalized
 position, therefore, due to the asset growth of the Company during 2005 and anticipated future growth, these trust preferred securities will be needed to help meet the capital needs.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company&#146;s total of $8.0 million of trust preferred securities are carried on the Company&#146;s consolidated balance sheet as subordinated debentures and the interest expense is recorded on the Company&#146;s consolidated statement of income. &nbsp;These trust preferred securities qualify as Tier 1 capital.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><BR></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:12pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Management of Interest Sensitivity</B></P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The Company&#146;s primary market risk exposure is interest rate risk and, to a lesser extent, liquidity risk. &nbsp;Interest rate risk is the exposure of the Company&#146;s financial condition to adverse movements in interest rates. &nbsp;The Company derives its income primarily from the excess of interest collected on its interest-earning assets over the interest paid on its interest-bearing liabilities. &nbsp;The rates of interest the Company earns on its assets and owes on its liabilities generally are established contractually for a period of time. &nbsp;Since market interest rates change over time, the Company is exposed to lower profitability if it cannot adapt to interest rate changes. &nbsp;Accepting interest rate risk can be an important source of profitability and shareholder value; however, excessive levels of interest rate risk could pose a significant threat to the Company&#146;s earnings and capital base. &nbsp;Accordingly, effective risk management that maintains interest rate risk at prudent levels is essential to the Company&#146;s safety and soundness. </P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Evaluating the exposure to changes in interest rates includes assessing both the adequacy of the process used to control interest rate risk and the quantitative level of exposure. &nbsp;The Bank&#146;s interest rate risk management process seeks to ensure that appropriate policies, procedures, management, information systems and internal controls are in place to maintain interest rate risk at prudent levels with consistency and continuity. &nbsp;In evaluating the quantitative level of interest rate risk, management assesses the existing and potential future effects of changes in interest rates on the Company&#146;s financial condition, including capital adequacy, earnings, liquidity, and asset quality.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Management uses three interest rate risk measurement techniques. &nbsp;The first, which is commonly referred to as GAP analysis, measures the difference between the dollar amounts of interest sensitive assets and liabilities that will be refinanced or re-priced during a given time period. &nbsp;A significant re-pricing gap could result in a negative impact to the Bank&#146;s net interest margin during periods of changing market interest rates. </P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The second interest rate risk measurement used is commonly referred to as net interest income simulation analysis. &nbsp;Management believes that this methodology provides a more accurate measurement of interest rate risk than the GAP analysis, and therefore, serves as one of its primary interest rate risk measurement techniques. &nbsp;The simulation model assesses the direction and magnitude of variations in net interest income resulting from potential changes in market interest rates. &nbsp;Key assumptions in the model include prepayment speeds on various loan and investment assets; cash flows and maturities of interest-sensitive assets and liabilities; and changes in market conditions impacting loan and deposit volume and pricing. &nbsp;These assumptions are inherently uncertain and subject to fluctuation and revision in a dynamic environment; therefore, the model cannot precisely estimate net interest income or exactly predict the impact of higher or lower interest rates on net interest income. &nbsp;Actual results will differ from simulated results due to timing, magnitude, and frequency of interest rate changes ,
 changes in market conditions ,
 and the Bank's strategies, among other factors.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Management conducted multiple simulations as of December 31, 2005, whereby it was assumed that a simultaneous, instant and sustained change in market interest rates occurred. &nbsp;Results of the simulation suggest that the Bank could expect net interest income to increase by approximately $318,000, if interest rates gradually decline by 100 basis points over the next twelve months, and to decrease approximately $306,000, if interest rates gradually increase 100 basis points over the next twelve months, from forecast levels of net interest income absent any changes in rates. &nbsp;These variances in net interest income were within the Bank&#146;s policy parameters established to manage interest rate risk. &nbsp;Other simulations are run quarterly looking at changes to net interest income given 200 and 300 basis point changes in interest rates. </P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The third interest rate risk measurement used is commonly referred to as an &#147;EVE&#148; analysis or an Economic Value of Equity analysis. &nbsp;Along with the simulation analysis, management believes that the EVE analysis methodology provides a more accurate measurement of interest rate risk than the GAP analysis, and therefore, serves as one of management&#146;s primary interest rate risk measurement techniques. &nbsp;The EVE analysis assesses the impact to the value of equity given potential changes in market interest rates. &nbsp;Key assumptions in the model include prepayment speeds on various loans, discount rates and the duration of assets and liabilities as determined by the federal regulatory agencies. &nbsp;These assumptions are inherently uncertain, subject to fluctuation and revision in a dynamic environment; therefore, the model cannot precisely estimate the value of equity or exactly predict the impact that higher or lower interest rates will have on the value of equity. &nbsp;Actual results will differ from simulated results due to timing, magnitude, and frequency of interest rate changes and changes in market conditions and the Company's strategies, among other factors.</P>
<P style="margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:11pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Results of the economic value of equity analysis done as of December 31, 2005, suggest that the Bank could expect the value of its equity to decrease 5.6% and 11.3%, if there was an immediate interest rate shift upward of 100 and 200 basis points and to increase 4.6% and 7.1%, if there was an immediate interest rate shift downward of 100 and 200 basis points. &nbsp;Management believes the different scenarios indicate a financial services organization that has relatively low overall interest rate risk.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">Austin Advisors, Inc., a firm specializing in consulting and providing assistance to banks, performs a formal asset/liability management analysis on a monthly basis. &nbsp;This information is presented and reviewed by the asset/liability committee.</P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; font-family:Times New Roman"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>Impact of Inflation and Changing Prices</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The majority of the Company&#146;s assets and liabilities are monetary in nature and therefore the Company has less exposure to inflation
 from most commercial and industrial companies that have significant investments in fixed assets or inventories. &nbsp;However, inflation does have an important impact on the growth of total assets in the banking industry and the resulting need to increase equity capital at higher than normal rates in order to maintain an appropriate equity to assets ratio. &nbsp;Inflation significantly affects non-interest expense, which tends to rise during periods of general inflation.</P>
<BR>
<BR>
<P style="page-break-before:always; margin-top:0pt; margin-bottom:-14pt; padding-left:72pt; text-indent:-72pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>ITEM 7A.</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; padding-left:72pt; line-height:14pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:12pt"><B>QUANTITATIVE AND QUANTATIVE DISCLOSURES ABOUT MARKET RISK .
</B></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:9pt"><BR></P>
<P style="margin:0pt; text-indent:36pt; line-height:13pt; font-family:Times New Roman; font-size:11pt">The following table provides information about the Company's financial instruments that are sensitive to changes in interest rates at December 31, 2005 and 2004. &nbsp;For loans receivable, securities, and liabilities with contractual maturities, the table presents principal cash flows and related weighted-average interest rates by contractual maturities. &nbsp;For core deposits (demand deposits, interest-bearing checking, savings, and money market deposits) that have no contractual maturity, the table presents principal cash flows and, as applicable, related weighted-average interest rates based upon the Company's historical experience, management's judgment, and statistical analysis concerning their most likely withdrawal behaviors. &nbsp;The current historical interest rates for core deposits are assumed to apply for future periods in this table as the actual interest rates that will need to be paid to maintain these deposits are not currently known. &nbsp;Weighted-average variable rates are based upon contractual rates existing at the reporting date.</P>
